wrote June 1, 1967 about demanding word, feeling ill, results of auto accident, getting my uniform tailored, problems of housesitting and a date for OC Fast.

8739 Linton Lane 
Alexandria, VA 22308

Dear Wally,

I’m really messed up tonight all day really sorry I didn’t write yesterday. At work, things have been terrible. Forget all I said about us being busy before. Nothing can compare to this week. Yesterday, after working hard all day – I decided to go to Colonel Madison‘s farewell party in the Zebra Room at Cameron. Well, I had one drink and about 10 cigarettes and felt horrible – not tight, just horrible so I come back here and went to bed – about eight and there I stayed until this. Morning at 7:30. Then back to work – and today was even worse. I had to do the monthly report +1,000,000 other things. Things were going from bad to worse when Sergeant Doherty snapped at me. It was so unusual because he is the least likely one to be rude to anyone. So I had to run from the office to keep from crying.

After that crisis passed, the insurance man came to see me at the office. I gave him my account of the accident – he had to prove that it wasn’t my fault so the company can close the case. This is my company, not the other guys. He said I would be contacted by the other insurance company in a few weeks and to have the damage of the car estimated so that I could give them that information when they called. He also said that my insurance isn’t adequate and if I were to get into an accident, that was my fault, the company wouldn’t pay. So I’ll call about more coverage tomorrow.

I took your uniform to that place whatever the name is, I forgot – and it will be ready in two weeks $9.50 for the stripes and $5.50 for the tapering.

Back home on the homefront, one of Terry’s fish died, and I turned the oil burner off by mistake and was afraid to turn it back on. The neighbor, John (Margaret’s husband came to my rescue– turned the burger on and took the dead fish out of the bowl.

God! I think that’s all it happened. I hope so. I miss you lots – I’m not lonely for Polly and Family, but it’s kind of cleared around here. Wish you were here.

I’ll see you Saturday. By the way, Carol a very cute girl at work, is interested in going to the party with Fast. See what he thinks.

Love,

Pat.

wrote June 21, 1967 about car repair, military uniform correction, quitting work, work effort.

8739 Linton Lane 
Alexandria, VA 22308

Dear Wally,

Brace yourself. The brake job cost $70.61. They put in new shoes, hose, wheel cylinders and master cylinder. If you remember, I had a master cylinder put in not three months ago at Sears. I’m taking down that receipt tomorrow and hope to be reimbursed for $15. I’ll bring today’s receipt Saturday so you can see what all they did. I got your letter in which you mentioned getting engineer buttons on the jacket. I called Lauterstein’s. They said they were sorry but they had so many people from Belvoir. It was just habit to put those buttons on the coats. He said he would put the ordinance buttons in the mail tonight.

Naturally, I’m dying to know what you learned from OPO today. I hope a letter is in the mail. I got paid today. $134 after taxes at all. One good thing, when I quit, I can withdraw the retirement funds.

Work is getting more hectic – everyone getting mad at each other it’s quite embarrassing sometimes to have two people in the office, shouting at each other and have a customer come in.

We’ll see you Saturday

Love Pat

Pat got a job with the Department of Defense, not far from an Army Depot where, as wife of a member of the Army, she could buy Army fatigues. The four sets issued me by Uncle Sam were nowhere near enough. Training in bad weather and mud, we Candidates might have to change midday. She got more for me and some of the guys in my part of the barracks. Eventually I had 16 sets.

Reveille  : It was nearly impossible to get out of bed, dash to the toilet, shave, dress in starch stiffened fatigues, jacket, and boots, make the bunk and get outside in formation in 15 minutes. I cut corners, by dressing for bed in long johns and socks, sleeping on top of my bunk, but under the spare blanket, in morning this left just the spare blanked to fold and the bunk to tuck tight. and the 15 minutes was in hand. Lot of shivering the first few nights, but unawares, I was training my body to generate more heat.

Many of the OCS classes had to do with military equipment needing frequent field repair or maintenance: the M48 Patton Tank, the M88 recovery vehicle, the four wheel drive truck, the M151 Jeep, the howitzer, the field gun, the machine gun, the rifle, field kitchen equipment, Barber-Greene Paving Machine, etc. Such were part of the bailiwick of my MOS  , 4815: Field Maintenance Platoon Leader. Most times, the training topic was bolstered with an appropriate training film. All uniformly erect in our seats, the film projector started, the lights went out and, had I ever gotten enough sleep, I would have learned how one pulls the diesel engine out of an M48 Tank, but instead I learned to fall asleep instantly, sitting bolt upright, and awaken instantly when the lights were turned back on. And if I were still dozey, Jim Endean would nudge me awake. An illicit cat-nap stolen out of view of TAC officers was a useful skill for my OCS survival, but it was years, post graduation, before I could go to a movie with Pat and see very much of the movie.

Some classes were practica. Although as officers we may never need to turn a wrench we really should have some hands on experience. Thus the Jeep Lab. Having overhauled my '55 Studebaker, I was comfortably ahead of my class. When the staff Sergeant served up automotive basics, sleep took over until I heard, "Candidate Bronstad, how does gasoline get from the fuel tank to the carburetor?" Cadre and class laughed at my half-awake response, "In 5 gallon cans."

Another practicum was to create our own memento of Ordnance. Maintenance companies had a metal lathe to quickly fabricate turned parts. Didn't feel bad my brass cannon was bested by Robert Jackson; He'd worked summers in his father's machine shop.

An Ordnance Corps Major taught the class on Explosive Ordnance Demolition He trembled alarmingly handling demonstration models and removing dummy detonators. Our best wag, Doug Ellis, said, "I wouldn't trust the major to unload a flashlight." Scuttlebutt held that the Major had a daughter with an incurable disease; only in the Army could he afford the medical care his daughter needed. By working in EOD, he got bonus pay for the added danger and could better afford extras for his daughter. The Army helps its own.

In one OCS class, the teachers required a book report, They wanted to insure minimal literacy, among other qualities, before issuing the certificate of rank. On rare leave I found Jean Lacouture's  Vietnam: The Lessons of War. (subsequently reprinted as Vietnam: Between two truces) Vietnam and war was probably the next, possibly last punch on my ticket, looked like "must reading". Lacouture convincingly argued the U.S. was there more by bull-blunder than foxlike craft. Fools get into restraining pens with bulls. The luster of gold bars began to tarnish before I could buy them. In the scores of years later, it became more and more clear that the Vietnam war was a colossal, death spattering blunder motivated by domestic American politics with only the pretense of national security.


wrote July 1, 1967 about coordinating with OC wives, doctor visit, work frustrations and Kinney's marriage.

8739 Linton Lane 
Alexandria, VA 22308

Dear Wally,

Well, I finally heard from all the wives – and it looks as if I won’t be there very early Saturday. Eydie [Endean] and Judy come in at 10:48 and Mavis at 11:15. So you can expect us about one or so.

I went to the doctor at DeWitt Army Hospital today – have a virus infection in my sinuses and bronchial tubes. They gave me all sorts of pills, cough syrup, and nose drops. I feel much better – even worked a half a day. And that whole half day was spent on the telephone tracing the shipment of one colonel. I called all over the East and Midwest and then South. It was frustrating, but I finally found it.

Kenny is getting married 5 August so Polly is getting ready to fly to Texas again.

I don’t count that note you sent me as a letter. Shame!

Love, Pat

Three or four times during the six months, we were required to rate each other, rate the potential of each as officers. Apparently test scores, P T scores and TAC officer evaluations were not enough. The Army still didn't really know what makes a good officer. The interpersonal component was needed too. It was a tool to let us chose, "who should be excluded from rank?" Most of us rated each other as demigods, but others not so exalted. Candidate Storey got blackballed, Froggy dinged another with the comment that "he looked like he slept in his uniform", which likely he did for the same reason I slept in long johns. Some candidates, failing in some measure, were retreaded, sent into classes that began two or more weeks later than ours. That prospect held it's own kind of horror; yet more weeks of OCS, back to more restrictive conditions, among strangers who were likely to select the retreaded candidate as one for blackballing! The Armed Forces operated under the enlightened management of Robert McNamara  who knew training could be viewed as just another manufacturing process, and evaluated by throughput. In McNamara metrics, better training units turned out nearly 100% of input.

Over the 6 months course of OCS, the relation of the OCS School to the Officer Candidates slowly shifted from theatrical carceral to thanks-for-affirming-our-effort collegial; we had to return to the world. Early June, on post, we could buy beer, we could buy newspapers. It stunned me to see the name of a fellow University of Michigan student on page one of the New York Times. He was an Air Force pilot, years ahead of me (in my abandoned academic aspirations), the golden haired boy of my academic adviser, Arthur Melton.

Melton had been a general in the Air Force or Army, before retiring and relentlessly driving himself in a purely academic (Experimental Psychology) career at Michigan. Captain Dale Noyd  , Ph.d. in hand, went on to a teaching post at the new Air Force Academy.

The NY Times filled in the unknown steps in Noyd's life. Needing pilots more than professors, the Air Force had reassigned Captain Noyd to train pilots heading to Vietnam. Noyd began to regard the U.S. role in the Vietnam War as immoral and illegal. He'd asked to be allowed to resign or to be classed as a conscientious objector. The Air Force would not make an exception. Thus the court martial.

My mental framework could not be restructured overnight. There is a lot of baggage in a 26 year old brain; if assigned to Vietnam, I probably could have killed a woman or child if I felt my life threatened. I admired Noyd, but was not up to his level of pluck. Luckily for me, the Army did not give me that test. I simply mourn for those so tested in Vietnam. Mourn and know the test was not necessary. (As A J P Taylor argues in The Origins of the Second World War, the fault for WW II lies less with Hitler's malignity and more with diplomatic blundering by Britain, France, and the U.S. Our received view of WW II, (like all wars), is distorted. It's largely that it is easier to sell history books that lay the blame on Germans than history books that deeply try to understand, "How can things go so colossally wrong?".)

I showed the newspaper to Froggy Bland, relating how I knew Noyd. Froggy must have thought I was going to follow Noyd; a few days later a TAC officer challenged the group wondering if any in our midst were going conscientious objector. Company commander Gorham wanted no stains on his record.

For more practice resurfacing into the real world, we had a formal party in Baltimore, Pat had Polly's help sewing her a gown. So long had I lived in the presence of healthy, robust men, It was a shock to see poor people who were not whole, people who were as fat as I am now, when Pat and I walked in downtown Baltimore. That perceptual distortion stayed with me for many years. The real world retained an element of the repellant.

Close to graduation, the final P T test. The command was, "Everyone will score at least 400." With continuous physical training in OCS, all were in better shape than they were in Basic Training when one had to score 300 to pass Basic, but there are real individual human limits. The "Honor Code" had been drilled into us, I knew I couldn't reach 400. TAC officers supervised, but they weren't enough to score all, we scored each other. One candidate lied for me to get a 400-adequate score for grenade throw, but I couldn't lie for Endean, I had yet to square the circle with inputs of command and truth. Later in my Army career, I learned that lies lubricate the machinery, permitting contradictories to collude. Indeed, at the Junior end of my Army career,

  • Lies lifted me,
  • lies lifted me,
  • when I feared I'd land in jail,
  • lies lifted me.

There were long established, institutional ways of dealing with problems like those I'd inherited from predecessor, Cpt Lynch. After much personal distress, the Brigade Commander showed me the Army way.



  Finally, graduation 19 July 1967.. I got the gold bars (brass ring). Pat packed up, left Polly, Kirk, Kinney, and Terry and drove the '59 Chevrolet up from Alexandria, Virginia and we took off to visit Bob in Wisconsin (via the UP), then our relatives in Fort Worth, then El Paso and Fort Bliss.

z321<^!-- not --> ✓



6248A Morgan, Fort Bliss, TX (~1967-08 — 1969-06)
Central reason for being in El Paso was Pat’s schooling.

Pat had begun college in 1964 at Eastern Michigan University in Ypsilanti, Michigan. On moving to Chicago in 1965 she resumed schooling at University of Illinois Chicago Circle. Next was North Texas University in Denton Texas, 1966. We hoped she could finish school at the University of Texas in El Paso, beginning 1967.

Pat needed the car for her commute, my requirements might find me miles out in the desert at the firing ranges. Not flush with money, my best recourse was a small motorcycle. At first a Honda 90. Too small for my ego but adequate to get there.

Pat quickly made friends at the University and we were as likely to have parties with university students as parties with officers and wives. Two of her University friends were Mike and Ginger McIntyre. Mike introduced me to cameras. When I tried to stabilize brigade property identities with photos it was with Mike’s Minox B  camera that I took property pictures. Then I learned film development and printing from Army photographers.

Jack and Marilyn Butler were two Army friends we maintained contact. The four of us partied together, went to Carlsbad Caverns and dined in Juarez. Jack and I explored the Organ mountains (just north of Franklin Mountain) on dirt bikes.

By summer ‘67, I knew I was not going to Vietnam. I’d called the Pentagon bureau responsible for reassignments, I was not going to Nam unless I chose to volunteer to go. Of course I had no desire to go. Pat would have to relocate. She could not stay in married housing if her husband were overseas.

1968

1968 was especially tumultuous, beginning in 30 January with North Vietnam’s Tet Offensive . Tet put the lie to administration war claims of “seeing light at the end of the tunnel”. Then followed, 3 months later, President Johnson announcing he would not run for another presidential term. The year 1968 was a time of riots, political turbulence, and mass civil unrest. The assassination of Martin Luther King in April of that year, following his opposition to the Vietnam War, further inflamed racial tensions, and protest riots in more than 100 cities followed.

During the 68 presidential primaries, Pat held a fundraiser for Senator Eugene McCarthy  at our army quarters. This effort was in part shared with fellow high school graduate Donna Griffin.

The singularly unruly Democratic National Convention also followed the assassination of Robert F. Kennedy, a candidate in the primary, on June 5.Currently second in delegates at the time of his death to the pro-war Humphrey, the loss of Kennedy saw his committed delegates go for Humphrey over candidate Eugene McCarthy, who had been third in delegates.

Whether the Nixon  campaign interfered with negotiations between the Johnson administration and the South Vietnamese by engaging Anna Chennault, a fundraiser for the Republican party, remains a controversy. It is not clear whether the government of South Vietnam needed encouragement to opt out of a peace process they considered disadvantageous.

In 1968, former vice president Richard M. Nixon won the presidency after promising that he would not continue to use the federal government to protect Black rights in the South. With that promise, racists made a permanent move from the Democrats to the Republicans, joining forces with businessmen who wanted an end to regulation and to taxes and the social welfare programs they funded. The race to tear apart the postwar government that helped all Americans by appealing to racism was on. I had disliked Richard M. Nixon, but developed a strong antipathy toward him, thinking that his prolongation of the Viet Nam war could only result in more deaths and could result in my period of service being extended possibly even having a tour of duty in Nam. Through the remainder of my life it has been hard to arrive at an even assessment of the man. His being at the top of my Chain of Command made my Army service morally repellant, whereas before, it had only seemed an obligation wrapped in tragedy.

A bonus of the Army’s, sending us to El Paso

Was finding our Fort Worth friends of five years ago in El Paso too. Mike and Eve Fox who then included Julie and Amy.

S Locust, Apt. 6M, Champaign, Illinois (06/14/69 — 08/69 )
I was an Illinois resident when drafted, so The University of Illinois...

After the pressure cookers of the military and school, we needed time to refind our lives. They had been interrupted by institutions. I could shed the uniform, grow a very non-military, bear-like beard but the underlying habits and tensions could not be shed so easily. Pat enjoyed a summer free of outside obligations.

wrote Jun., 1969 of Champaign, IL, bad weather, cats Blackie, Brownie and Chewey. +Context

1969 June

802 South Locust, Apt. 6M

Champaign, IL, 61820

Dear Mike & Eve,

We are alive at 802 South Locust, apartment 6M, Champaign, Illinois, 61820. I asked Walt if he had anything special to say – he said, ask for money.

After leaving Fort Worth, we went to Wisconsin, then to Champaign, where we’ll be encamped for seven more weeks. The climate here is terrible - it has already rained in the two weeks. We’ve been here – more than El Paso sees in three years. The rain I don’t mind, it’s just the jungle heat – never thought I’d miss the Sun City barf.

Did you know that Mickey Mouse wears a Spiro Agnew wrist watch?

I hope you are all there well.

We left Blacky and Brownie to spend the summer in Fort Worth, and brought only Chewy with us.

Walt’s growing a beard - how did you ever adjust to a beard, Eve? Does it ever get softer?

I hope to hear from you soon. Our telephone number is 356–372 in case you get an urge to call.

Love. Pat.

Cutting my enlistment short by two months may have been more important than graduate school. The Viet Nam war had grown more and more objectionable; that Richard M Nixon had ascended to the top of the Chain of Command was simply intolerable.

1970 May

351 B Longshore Dr

Ann Arbor, MI 48104

Dear Mike & Eve,

I hope you’re still at this address – it’s been so long since I’ve written, It’s hard to tell.

Haven’t written cause things stay the same around here. Walt goes to school, I go to work; on the weekends, we drink beer and talk about the studies and the work done during the week. Not very exciting. But we are both glad to be in Ann Arbor – and, good God sometimes we even miss El Paso.

How’s Mike’s progress? Are you in school?

I wish you didn’t have the bad habit of writing only when you’re written to. That’s not fair. How are we supposed to know what’s going on.

We have Chewy and Brownie with us (Blackie’s with the Bronstad‘s). Chewy and Browne get along beautifully together since Browns been “fixed.“ Only thing is that his hair is fallen out in big splotches. Where he has hair, it’s long and shiny, but baldness really doesn’t become him.

We are very, very close to buying a small house of a friend who is leaving. It’s really just a winterized lake cottage on Whitmore Lake (exactly 9.4 miles from campus). It has two bedrooms and looks sort of like this [illustration] Makes your house on Signal Peak [El Paso] look big. Has a dark room in the basement. Has a garage and a nice sized (?) (big compared to house) corner lot with lots of trees – on a lake where swimming, fishing, etc. is allowed.

So if the deal goes through, we cut payments from $150 (rent) to $50 (house payments). Sounds great to us – and no neighbors attacking our eardrums 24 hours a day. {of course though we will have a loan payment of $60 a month, but it’s still cheaper}

Please do write and tell us what’s going on – how the kids are, etc.

Love, Pat

z607 ✓

Grove, Box 192 Whitmore Lk, MI (~1970-06 — 1974-11)
Postponed Parenthood.
1970-1985

We hadn’t anticipated owning a house so soon, but Jim and Linda Taylor needed to sell their home, and home ownership seemed like such a good idea that, why not?

I wasn’t happy about some of the features of semi rural living. 362 Grove required its own water supply, having a shallow well and veteran pump and pressure tank, it required its own septic service and a drain-field; it was surrounded by tall trees, many of which could fall on the house (one did); the house was initially constructed as a summertime, lakeside getaway, later retrofitted with an oil tank, furnace and some insulation for year-round occupation. The water was so mineral laden that the metal shower stall and the plastic curtain were permanently colored a rose-golden tan. Jim had made polyethylene storm windows for the enclosed front porch that one really needed for year-round occupancy of the front porch/office.

There were some unique pluses; Jim had added a photographic dark room to the basement’s water pump, hot water heater, furnace access space. And he would let go of his Czech made, Meopta Opemus II photo enlarger and other dark room equipment cheap. The one car garage, unlike the house, was relatively spacious. It could hold our car, motorcycle, and trailer when the trailer was tipped-up on its end.

Jim and Linda had refinished a round oak dining table and spindle back chairs. Pat and I have had the table and chairs ever since - including two crossings of the equator. There were other student level furnishings, most of which failed to make one of our seven crucial moves since 362 Grove.

Some friendships conveyed with the house: the elderly couple Jim and Louise Townsend, our only property line neighbor. Across the access street lived Jim and Pee-wee Gilliam who were generous in sharing the woodland knowledge Jim had picked up in West Virginia. Other neighbors were friendly to varying degrees but we had less attachment.


Finally, there was Boo the dog who conveyed too; I think Boo may have come with the house when Jim and Linda bought it. Boo was shaggy enough to endure Michigan winters; she had a house with abundant oak leaves banked up against it. While Boo was adequate to the winters we were ignorant of her vulnerability to heartworm which claimed Boo’s life in ‘72 when the Vet’s cure, as warned, prove fatal.

wrote June, 1970 of Boo & Pi, tulips, violets, hyacinths, daffodils, DC protest and politics. +Context

362 Grove, Box 192
Whitmore Lk, MI 48189

8316 Signal Peak
El Paso, TX 79904

Dear Mike & Eve,

We didn’t have enough animals before, so we acquired two more. A huge dog and an almost as huge cat came with the house we bought. Boo the dog, is not a very good watchdog, but she really attacks the scraps from the table and any crumbs she finds while she’s running around the house ant-eater style. Pi can’t get used to being the cat around here. She only comes around at meal time and she eats very fast, grumbling the whole time.

We’ve been here almost 2 weeks now – have planted even more flowers. The tulips, violets, hyacinth, and daffodils are in bloom, it is really quiet and peaceful here though we hardly been around much. We just got back from Washington. [D. C., attending a Viet Nam War protest.]

The mood there this time was very subdued, almost apathetic. I guess no one really had any confidence that the protest would do any good, but just couldn’t think of any other method. We came away more depressed than ever.

The primary in Texas was really a shock – though I guess it shouldn’t have been. It sounds as if Michigan will be just as well represented as you – I have a real fear that Mrs. Romney will win over Senator Hart. Add her to Griffin – and Gerald Ford – now I’m really depressed!

Write to tell us how you’re doing, etc.

Love, Pat

Walt says hello.

Almost forgot – Walt ran into Paul Grosser who is here attending a poli-sci seminar. He’s teaching at LSU now – said Jim Peterson went bankrupt.

Walt just talked to him briefly – and hasn’t seen him since, but Paul is planning on spending the summer here – his wife is to join him shortly. We’ve talked about inviting them out – but I don’t know if we will – even want to. it would be sort of a revival of many things pleasantly or finally forgotten. Sorry I write so seldomly – do let us hear from you

Love Pat

PS Marilyn Butler may visit us with her new Jewish beau.

Letter's Context

2026-10-21

Babies meant diapers and lots of clothes washing. I spent much of that summer extending hot and cold water lines deep underground from the house to the garage. In the garage, I installed hot and cold water, a washing machine, and an insullating enclosere in the garage to keep machine and water lines from freezing. For really cold nights, I turned on a light in the enclosure.

Ron Fisher, who grew up on a Saskatchewan farm explained that wet clothes would dry in coldest weather, hung outdoors. Just took a little longer.

Letter's Context

2026-10-19

Photography was a pleasant diversion from academics.

The OBGYN forbade flash photography; too much risk of temporatily blinding delivery personnel at a critical time. So I used GAF film pushed to ASA 800, highest film speed easily available at the time.

Years later, the GAF film showed its shortcomings, slides spontaneously sported pepto-Bismol pink spots.

wrote Oct 27, 1972 to Louise and Clyde of her gratitude to her Mother Clara. +Context

362 Grove, Box 192
Whitmore Lk, MI 48189

4710 E Loraine
Ft Worth, TX

Dear Gramps & Granny

My mom & dad told me all about you and I can’t wait to meet you. I met my other granny; she sure knows how to handle a baby – not like two other people I could mention. Now take the daily bath she gave me - Never missed an inch. The day after she left, my mom finally got a chance and she blew it. The first day she forgot to wash my back — the whole back. Next day, she missed my left foot! On Wednesday she was so nervous, she forgot to bathe me at all — so I had to go to the doctor with a dirty bod and even dirtier back and foot. Boy was I embarrassed.

The trip to the doctor was something else. All Mom & Dad had talked about since I got home was the visit — and were always writing things down on a big sheet of paper. You’d think taking care of me was difficult or something. Anyway, all three of us went (I was the only calm one). They let a nurse and two doctors punch & probe me for nearly 2 hours — and found out what I could’ve told them before we even went, that I’m perfectly healthy! For three weeks, they had been trying to decide if I were gaining enough weight — found out I had gained 2 lbs. 6 oz. — and were then worried that I had gained too much! I can’t seem to do anything right around here. For instance, Mom complains about getting up in the middle of the night to feed me so last night I decided to sleep all night (only until seven though can’t let her get too much rest). But it was very hard to sleep. All night long I could hear them tiptoe into my room to make sure I was still breathing. (I’ll admit I did try out my shallow breathing techniques on them a couple of times). But anyway, guess I won’t try that again, it’s too tiring for me.

Mom & Dad were really excited when they saw these baby pictures you sent — said I look just like Dad. Well, maybe, but my cheeks certainly aren’t that fat! They especially like the one where dad is holding the rabbit. I was sort of bored by it, but I sure did like that bunny. Tell Aunt Bette she sure looks like a cutie from that picture I saw of her with dad.

I just heard them settling down so I better get busy and figure out a way to get them in here. I found if I let them get too comfortable, it takes a lot longer to rouse them.

With love your grandson, Philip

362 Grove, Box 192

One of the cultural twists of the time was "Blacksploitation" films. Mike's lifelong ambition to be a writer found temporary footing writing for a Texas oil scion who wanted to get in on the exploitative windfall. Mike wrote scenes and dialog for "Fox Style", and acted in many scenes, but the opus was born of no artistic impulse or craft. The Fox wrote Feb. 27, 1973 of income from writing, travel camper, old man's weed, future story, therapist, Pat and Philip..

2612 Canton Dr 
Ft Worth, TX 79904

362 Grove
Whitmore Lake, MI

Saturday afternoon, February something

Contrary to opinions I am not dead. The story I was working on when you were here was grinding down, anyway. Then my agent Proqueen called to say she had a backer who wanted to put down $200,000 to produce a story I had just finished the time you came before. So I am pushing out pages to get a rewrite done by next week. I should be working on it, but I am laying up to work like hell next week.

This one is the only serious thing I’ve done so far. Bleak, violent, almost devoid of hope – negative to match my mood, the mood of us both, the first year back in Fort Worth. I talked to my Dallas person this morning, and now United Artist wants in on it. This one is a money crop, enough to last us through writing another good one. With this, if it happens, we get an old van, panel it inside, put in seat, beds, get a tent, stove, lanterns, and other goodies and travel the summer away. The nature of this business is a gamble. It makes it rough and heartbreaking or pure ego freak out. It’s as far as one can get from White Sands Missile Range.

I wrote you another letter, but it was before the ship came in, therefore full of meanness and grouchy sermons. I can’t find much in it worth rewriting.


A theory put forward into the form of a tale.


Grass possesses not one effect, but two – in the new, freshly harvested leaf, there is an enormous effect on the body, and an accompanying mild effect in the head. The older it gets, i.e. mellower, the less effect there is on the bod, the more effect in the head.

My old man bought a lid from his apprentice down at the shop. He gave it to me because he doesn’t like his throat to be dry. He is new to grass so anything Green will stone the shit out of him. I look at it, smell it, crumble it – street shit. I pick out a leaf from a pinon pine. South Texas, Northern New Mexico – good growing country, but definitely not great. Street shit nonetheless, took it; shifted it picked out the detritus, leaves, dog shit, rolled a teeny little number, just to check how bad the old man got ripped off on a $10 lid. 15 minutes pass. Nothing zilch. Street shit. 30 minutes nothing. No effects, nothing.

45 minutes – I am sitting in the rocking chair watching TV. I rock slightly and am engulfed by nausea. I sit still, and my head rolls off on the floor. Getting up to retrieve it, I walk backwards our sideways. The Tom cat starts laughing at me. Auditory hallucinations, color, trips, dizziness, slurred, speech, the whole physical, peyote trip, except no puking until 3:30 in the morning when I finally got my eyes to close. Included with this was a mild, irrational, headstone, new grass, like when we bought in our crop year before last, and found ourselves deified by the Arlington freaks we gave complementary baggies too. I bought a second lid from the old man’s apprentice.

I’m thinking of doing a story on an old Indian con artist out in Arizona, who makes his living from from groceries clothes, money, etc. contributed by ernest somewhat dense graduate students. He is stringing along any number of them, but they think they alone know him. When one is coming in the front yard with a load of groceries, he has another in the back room sticking live grasshoppers in his ears and nose; another is just going out the back to look for magic sky hooks; another is out on a mountain top learning to fart colored bubbles. Another is by a lake hopping on 1 foot, naked, chanting “George Washington fuck a duck“. He keeps em moving and they keep him living well. They think the element of his constant laughter is the key to his philosophy. It is.


Tuesday –

Thomas still has his bold patch under his armpit, but he recovered in no time. Nights he is out on business, days he plops on his furry butt. I don’t know why Thomas is so important to us. But he seems to understand and takes full advantage of it. It would have been catastrophic if we had lost affable Thomas.

I want to know more about this analyst counselor – shrink broad you keep raving about. We’re rotting the mind of ours with National Lampoon, dope, Don Juan, etc. The liberal weltschmertz is starting to get to him, but you sound like yours has the upper hand. Nobody has the shit together that good in this day in time, unless they’re a mental basket case, which of course qualifies them for a cabinet post. Next time you see her, tell her you have an atheist friend in Texas – one who doesn’t believe mental health is healthy. Keep fighting. All you have at stake is the right to be screwed up according to other people’s opinions, but sane in your own. Unamerican you know.

I didn’t get a chance to engage in any heavy rap with Philip or Pat; they seem to be pretty heavy with each other, which is good. Don’t feel deprived and left out in all that. Just wait until weaning time; that’s when the fun begins. The script is lagging and we have to go apply for food stamps, and I’m out of note paper. So I will finally mail this thing. With luck and extortion we’ll see you late spring or summer Michael

PS it ain’t much of a letter; but I haven’t written one in 10 years.

Write.

wrote Jan. 13, 1974 of warming house, Philip and his behavior —Za5

362 Grove.
Whitmore Lk, MI

4710 E Loraine
Fort Worth, TX

Dear Folks,

You said you would like to meet Philip, why not come here for a week or two? I know it’s rather cold, but we could turn the heat up for such a short period; you could spend lots of time wrapped up in the chair, our sofa; you could sleep in the living room, That’s the warmest room in the house. There’s no worry about strange food or dangerous water, just close up the house and spend a couple of weeks here.

While I can’t predict how Philip would react; he seems to react best when people let him come to them; That is people who just accept his presence and go on about their business seem to get his attention faster than those who press. He’s walking and running about pretty well now, if there’s no obstruction on the floor in front of him, he’ll run with pretty good control, arms stretched out at about 45° anticipating the almost inevitable fall. Poor little guy, some days he sustained three or four bruises on his much battered head; he just doesn’t seem to understand slowing down. About four days ago, we were at a friend’s house, Philip, Mikey, and me down in the basement. Philip became fascinated with the cat, which was hiding at the top of the stairs. He crawled up about six times, intimidating, the poor, cowering cat, but some of those times, when he was ready to come down, he would just leap down toward me, overconfident that I would be ready to catch him.

Not much speech yet. “No”, “dog barks”, and some very imperfect repetitions are about all I can understand. His passive understanding is pretty good perhaps not as good as we assume; sometimes I launch into long involved, explanations, little of which he is equipped to understand. But he understands “go to your highchair“ when he’s hungry. He understands putting things back when he gets something out although the putting of it back may exceed his ability. –

All in all, Philip is a beautiful little boy; he may be too fond of pulling hair and biting in moments of ecstasy, and indifferent about our noses and eyes when his curiosity is directed toward them, but he can be spontaneously, affectionate, offering us a pat on the head for good behavior, part of a soggy cookie when he has had just about enough.

I haven’t done many prints yet; I’ve enclosed contacts of grandpa Embrey, I’ll make some more and return the negatives later. How many would you like? You can trim the contacts as you like.

Good luck with your mineral hopes. Right and tell us if you intend to come. - Love Walton.

362 Grove, Box 192

Dear Eve,

What do you mean – “thanks for the short note …“ – that was a letter – a long one. Everyone is asleep around here – Walt works all night, so, of course, he’s sleeping. Philip finally ran out of steam and pooped out on the couch. If I could find a convenient place, I join them.

We will be leaving here Wednesday morning – early I hope. We’ll stop at a friends house in Memphis, then at my sister’s in Roanoke (she and Family just moved there from Washington DC – my little sister, Betty, who I haven’t seen in about four years. Then we’ll head for Fort Worth. We haven’t told the Bronstads the date yet, but I did tell them that we wouldn’t spend all of our time with them – that we’d spend time with other relatives and friends – they weren’t too happy, but said they understood.

I’d really like to move there, but don’t know if the reasons I give, all the real ones – maybe I’m running away from something here – who knows. My mind these days does double somersaults constantly.

I am really glad I called and talked to you and Mike – made me feel better – for a while – then great doubts and clouds started festering again – maybe I’m the one who should be in therapy. I read a book the other day about this totally in perceptive, completely selfish person who had no idea that she was in perceptive and selfish – she thought she was really understanding and a great judge of people and and and – and. I just got to thinking - not for the first time – that perhaps that’s me – causing chaos wherever I go. Hurting people and not even being aware of it. If that’s me, I would like to change - but how do I go about it – clouds, clouds festering. Oh shit! I never even told Walt I called you - I said too many things that I didn’t want to repeat to him. I do have a tendency to say too much about other peoples confidences.

Anyway – see you soon. is this a short note?

Love, Pat.

Dear Eve,

It’s very depressing at times.

And now I have a horrible head cold – my nose hurts, my eyes hurt, am I cheering you. I liked Eric Severide’s comments last night – after Jerry Ford‘s press conference – that the Ford administration had gotten off to a running stop.

Philip talks all the time – making sentences and demands like a machine gun. the other morning, I asked him if he wanted to potty; he said, “no, I want to pee on mama. “Very funny kid – except that at the time he was saying it, he was doing just that.

How‘s Mike these days? I wish we were there – I think I could help him in his land – scaping ventures – or it must be just wishful thinking, but I do have lots of ideas.

Love to you all, please write.

Pat

Happy birthday to Amy – sorry I never remember their birthdays write them down for me. I know yours, so give me Amy’s, Julie‘s and Mike’s.

wrote Oct., 1974 of Walt's parents, rug hooking, family connections, Ft Worth house market. +Context

362 Grove, Box 192
Whitmore Lk, MI 48189

2114 Tierney Ave.
Ft Worth, TX

Dear Eve,

Walt’s parents were here for a visit – just left yesterday. We enjoyed having them here; I turned his mom onto rug hooking. I am washing and cutting the wool for her pattern now. It’s fun. By the way, save all the old flannel wool, like 50 cent of skirt, church sales, garage sales cheap you can get. It’s much cheaper to prepare the wool yourself than buying it already prepared. Then not only do you save money, you can dye the colors you want. And it’s really exciting to do the whole thing. Louise and I are going to buy a cutting machine (cut wool strips for hooking) so we all should be set to have a relatively cheap hobby. I thought I would get you a needle and a hook – unless you have a large one with the screw adjustment let me know.

We have our house for sale – so far only one family has looked – and we haven’t heard again from them. Whenever we can unload it, we will be down. No, you and Mike did not unduly influence us. Of course, we will be delighted to be living close to you again, but it’s just that there is really no reason to stay here any longer and we would like Philip to know his relatives. I think kids miss a lot if they grow up not being part of an extended family. I have really fond memories of my grandmother – and assorted aunts and uncles – Walt too – and we would like for Philip to have the same kind of relationships that were important to us.

Walt too worries about his folks – they are getting old – anyway, you are a reason we are moving back but not the. Feel better? Or worse?

Also, when I get there, I want to get a kiln and make pottery.

We would like a house on the east side, a big one with a fireplace – an older house on a large lot with lots of trees – big rooms, a house, not unlike yours. the price can vary from 15,000 to 25,000 – cause we’ll have the money from this house to invest – maybe even as high as 30,000 if it’s a wonderful old thing we just can’t pass up. You might look around but I have no idea when we’ll come – depends on when we sell this. if you see something you know we’d love – call us right away. Big kitchen lots of windows lots of light.

Do write – hope you enjoy the pictures. Let me know about hoop.

Remember about wool – flannel

Love

Pat, Walt and Phillips send their love too.

Dear Eve,

Haven’t heard anything yet – but it’s just Monday. I’m really itching to leave. As you may have guessed by now.

I let my bangs grow out so now I have a new look from [drawing medium hair length] to [drawing longer hair length]– sexy huh?

I can’t decide if I like it or not – can’t stand all that glamour! –

Philip is still a stinker – and is funny – the things he says. Did I tell you one morning he got up earlier than we did, came into the bedroom and started saying – “mommy, daddy”. He repeated that about five times, but our only responses were sleepy grunts and groans. Finally he said, “coffee ready, mama“. He saw a picture of a little girl the other day and called it Amy.

Anyway, we’re still here – still looking forward to coming – and will see you then. Please do write and call – I love that! Walt too – he never seems to be here when you and we talk.

Love,

Pat

One Wackenhut Security job had me at an Ann Arbor Elementary School refurbishing site; guarding it at night.I salvaged some pieces of wonderful old growth oak that had been used for railings . Letter's Context

2026-10-29

In El Paso, after Pat graduated UTEP, she wasnted a sewing machine. In past, she had spent time sewing this or that emblem on my Army fatigues. All of her family sewed, so I expect she thought a sewing machine one of the electric tools for women as assuredly as a man might want a skill saw. But Pat's portable Singer sewing machine saw little use; Pat was a woman who would much rather read a book than make a dress.

Many things were useless. The tiny sailboat inherited from Jim and Linda. I took it out not more than twice. My motorcycle died months ago; I sold it for salvage parts. The trailer used to carry motorcycle and goods from El Paso to Ann Arbor had too little capacity for our grown household.

I planned to rent a U-Haul truck and tow the Plymouth Valiant behind it. The car cabin could become a greenhouse for our houseplants. On the trip from Michigan to Texas, passers-by gave us big smiles and thumbs up, probably mistaking the False Aralia in the trailing Valiant for Marijuana.

Nine months passed between the 1974 November and the 1975 August letters. In that interval Pat, Walt and Matt lived in different places.


Beginning November, 1974 to March 1975 we lived at 4710 E Loraine St, Fort Worth, TX. Pat, Matt and I crammed into the back bedroom of my Father's house. I was so disaffected by my academic crash that I sought out manual labor. I tried an apprenticeship with a rock mechanic. I still have the Estwing rock hammer I bought. The 80 mile round trip to the last job site on Lake Granbury ate into apprentice pay. I didn't fit in; I was replaced by a man who had done work study rock work in one of Texas' Penitentiaries. Fair enough, I had more options than he did. Next, I built barbed wire fence on My Uncle Embrey's land in Hamilton County. I took Bette's friend Rael as a helper; this was harder work, poorer pay and a longer commute. Like Army Basic, it was good experience, one did not want to repeat. I still have the fencing pliers I bought. I did a one-off gig to move my Aunt Aubrey from the Hamilton address to her Hico address. Friction between me and my sister Bette caused Pat, Matt, and me to move on to the Tierney house.

In April 1975 to June 1975 we three lived in the granny flat behind Mike and Eve's home at 2114 Tierney Ave, Fort Worth, TX. These were slightly larger accommodations

Mike had a go at landscaping. He had a crew of his nephew, Matthew, Rusty Boyd and now me. Mike got the gigs by advertising in the Fort Worth Star Telegram classifieds, and then we'd go rake, mow, trim, whatever. And when Mike got no gigs, we'd smoke pot in his breakfast room.

Leslie Kish had urged me to have a fallback; he pointed me toward a U M course in programming. It was demanding; I'd be at the North Campus until one or two A. M., working on class assignments.

Programming was a skill I could put to use. Having had my fill of manual labor, I got a job with the Veterans Administration in Austin. My Father, Clyde, who had trained at Camp Mabry [Austin] during the Great War wanted to see Austin; he accompanied me to my job interview.

From July 1975 to October 1975 we rented 100 B Florence, Austin, TX. These were larger accommodations and a walk away from Pat's Uncle Frank and Aunt Gail Bird at 1014 E 53rd St.

Mike and Vi Jurica's adobe house was probably older than the United States. The Juricas continue to adapt it to their tastes, respecting the wisdom embedded in the structure, yet unafraid to express themselves.

Five years in Michigan made me acutely aware of the need for insulation. To poke paper backed spun glass insullation into the cramped wedge of the low sloped roof, I made a tool of a clipboard, stick and release string to angle insulation deep where I could not crawl.

Jim Taylor sold me his dark room equipment; he couldn't take it to England. I'd been introduced to film developing and printing in the Army. The 362 Grove basement held the darkroom (along with the household water pump, storage tank and water heater.) I'd carried the equipment from Michigan to Texas, to New Zeland, to Oregon back to Texas. Only before the move to Tennessee did I let go of it, always unused except in Michigan.

At 1721 Norris, I'd intended to build another darkroom; it never materialized.

1721 Norris

Dear Eve,

We didn’t make it to Fort Worth as you may have noticed – many reasons – too hot – plumbing backed up, too much company last week (Bronstad came Saturday, left Tuesday a.m. – Birds came Tuesday a.m. left Thursday p.m.) We enjoyed all [of] it, but we’re glad to be just 5 again.

2026-10-29

Don't clearly remember the Bronstad's visits; their visits were frequent enough to lose distinctiveness after 46 years. Bird visits were rarer. I think the visitors were two of Pat's sisters, Iva (Potsy Bird) Bryant, Frances (Sissy Bird) Shivers and Clara Bird, Pat's Mother. Dear Margaret (Piggie Bird) Norwood was always so generous with food.

I do remember wondering how we could accommodate the Birds. I attempted to make a sofa like platform out of plywood, assuming I could get foam cushiions and cover it. My attempt was a total failure; somehow we worked two nights of sleep all out.

My waterbed construction project was simpler and more successful; just four 2" x 12"'s hinged together with door hinges. (The better to disassemble for moving.)

On amused reflection, it seems like I've made a lot of sleeping accomodations. The headboard for out marriage bed at the Sylvania address. The sleeping platform for 362 Grove (just four added inches of elevation, but that helped a lot in Michigan winters sleeping on a 4" foam pad atop an uninsulated floor). The pine waterbed for Austin, the rimu waterbed for Karori. The failed attempt for the Bird visit, not to mention the storage / bed monstrosity I made for Father and Mother. They dutifully slept on it for years.

Philip goes to the park tomorrow – it’s the last day of summer school. We have decided to send him back to the same place for the next year. I think he’ll like it a lot better than the Montessori school where he’s been going.

The girls did not have any teeth yesterday. Kris has two and Kate one – and each has at least four more ready to pop out through any minute. Let me tell you it’s holy hell having two babies cutting 12 teeth all at one time. Help! They also can walk a few steps – or would until they stopped everything except crying – two days ago to work on their teeth.

Walt‘s been busy insulating the house – our fuel bills are out of sight.

Had a strange dream about Karen the other night.

How’s the landscape business? Tell me what it’s like going out every day. You think you could come some weekend soon? We’d like to see you!

We got the card about Sesame Street magazine. Thanks.

Love.

Pat.

A work colleague, George King, who had begun Austin Veterans Administration work in the same 1975, 13 week training class, had bought a model home in Austin. The model home developer had converted the home's two car garage into sales office space, using four sets of sliding glass doors, two six foot and two eight foot. George had no need of an office, but he did want a garage for his cars. He wanted to be rid of the doors and I had the idea to enclose the front porch with the doors. I removed the Austin stone verneer from the enclosure, using the stone for corner posts at the supporting wrought iron columns. Then I put gypsum bord on the exterior-become-interior, wall papered it and laid tile on the slab floor continuing the illusion that what was once a front porch was now "interior"

wrote Nov. 25, 1985 of rain, new house and car, Bryan and Pat Tyler, gossip and school. +Context

123 Campbell Ave.
Karori, Wellington 5, New Zealand

2114 Tierney Ave.
Ft Worth, TX

Dear Eve,

It’s been raining here all day – and yesterday – I didn’t know there was so much rain! It is spring so I guess it’s usual, but it sure ruins a Sunday .

We are now the proud owners of a house and a car. We’ve had the car about three weeks (a red 81 Toyota Corolla) and I’m still not driving very often. These people drive on the other side of the road and when it comes time to make a right turn, I get totally confused. We move into our house on 5 December and are really looking forward to that. The house is very modern in design - (It’s about 10 years old) and is on a hillside – so while we don’t have a harbor view, we do have a terrific view of Karori [Matt outside on dining room deck with Karori in background]. (one of these days we’ll take some pictures). It’s going to be hard to move though – the people next-door are just great. It’ll be sad to move away from Bryan and Pat. Pat and I run in the park every day – or did until she had a hysterectomy – so now I run with her dog Tina and await Pat’s return to the trail.

We are not moving very far away – the kids will still go to the same school – in fact, we can see the school from our new house.

The real estate lady who has sold us the house – and show us many others – invited me over to her house to meet her women friends, and her mother, (her husband is in the states on business right now). I had such a wonderful time – didn’t realize how much I missed gossip! The ladies talked about the Prime Minister‘s wife who didn’t (doesn’t) take care of him, the new Governor-General who’s part Māori, about well you know just junk, but I had missed that. I had a great time but had to watch the wine since it was the first time I had been out in the car by myself. I think the main reason Gillian invited me over was to see me in something besides jeans.(I don’t part with my past quickly). She seems satisfied that I did indeed have legs.

The kids get out of school (end of school year) on 13 December. They seem to have adjusted very well and have quite a few friends. Matthew goes to the social for kids his age. (in the neighborhood) at the Anglican church every Friday night. Kate and Kris start swimming lessons on Monday as part of their school day. They are all looking forward to the end of the school parties.

Our address after December will be

15 Lynmouth Ave.

Karori;

Wellington 5

New Zealand

I am surprised at the number of succulent plants here – everywhere there are Christmas cactuses, donkey tails – and all kinds of cacti. Somehow, I didn’t expect that. Best wishes for Thanksgiving (not one here – but we may have a turkey dinner – if I can find a turkey)

Love

Pat .

Letter's Context

2026-10-29

We've moved to New Zealand. After years of dissatisfacction with President Ronald Reagan, and years of increased nuclear tensions, Pat and I concurred that we wanted to get as far away as we comfortably could from an area of likely nuclear attack. Austin had Bergstrom Air base near by plus some high tech industry and a major highway artery.

The very first few days were quite jarring. Databank had generously allowed us a two day stay in Hawaii, pleasantly cooler than the July the Austin we'd left. Cold, gale force winds persuaded us that in New Zealand, July means winter. Our bodies would not accept that autumn could last less than a day, traversing the Equater and Tropic of Capicorn by air.

For the first few months, Databank covered our housing cost in a Motel in Lower Hutt at 95-98 Pharazyn St. Databank were getting us on our way to cultural conversion by loading our initial larder with stuff like Marmite. The rail stop was a short walk away where I could commute to Downtown Wellingon on the narrow gauge train. Lower Hutt was sandwiched between Upper Hutt and Petone along the Hutt river.

Pat had to walk across the river to buy groceries and carry them back withut the convenience of a car.


Wellington is New Zealand's capitol, the nation has roughly 1/100 the population of the United States. Consequently central political figures were treated more casually, both by themselves and the populace. For example, I rubbed elbows with a Prime Minister while browsing at the garden store.

wrote Jan 2, 1986 of new home, new cat, Kate, Kris, Matthew, Walt. +Context

15 Lynmouth Ave..
Karori, Wellington 5, New Zealand

2114 Tierney Ave.
Ft Worth, TX

Dear Eve,

Well, another year gone – and I feel no wiser, do you?

It is summer now – early summer – and I’m finally beginning to believe it (my mind says Winter still). When the sun is out, it is just beautiful here – and I feel like a lizard crawling out to its warmth. It’s a little windy today. (it does get windy here – part of Wellington‘s charm which I may understand someday). It seems so unlike January – but then August didn’t seem like August either. One of these days my calendar will be in sync.

We love our house. It’s about 10 years old – and roomy, light, and warm. (It’s the first year we’ve had a fireplace to hang stockings by.) Walt is making a frame for a waterbed out of a native wood called Remu – it is very pretty and even put a headboard on it this time. he’s building the platform now, so hopefully we’ll be sleeping on it tonight. I finally got those oak chairs (the ones we had in the kitchen) sanded and varnished. They look great.

We have a new cat – well not new, secondhand I guess. We met a family who is moving to Malaysia and couldn’t take their nine month old cat Toffee. So after checking us out, they decided Toffee could adopt us. He’s a real sweet cat and has quite a loud purr.

The kids are out of school until February 4. Kate and Kris will be in Standard 3 at Karori Normal and Matthew will be in Form 3 at Wellington College.

Walt rides his bike to work every day – it’s a little harder since we moved up to this hill. Running is harder too, but I’ve gotten back to it – run far enough so that I can walk back up the hill instead of running.

Christmas Day was quiet here, but we did have a nice day. We spent the afternoon at the Tyler‘s (our neighbors on Campbell Street) both our old neighbors (Tyler’s) and our new ones (Crumps) got the kids presents – nothing big, but I thought that was very nice. K & K & M thought it was wonderful, of course. We went to a Databank picnic the week before Christmas. Father Christmas arrived in a jeep with presents for all the kids. Then there was a party at Walt’s office and the kids were given another present! Walt gave me a green stone (native Jade) necklace –

Enclosed (I hope) are a couple of pictures. Thanks for the one of Amy. There is a shelf in our bedroom where I put all the pictures of family and friends. Why don’t you send one of you and Mike?

How are Rusty and his wife Lisa doing?

We are doing fine – we miss everyone though,

Love.

Pat

Letter's Context

2026-10-29

Seasons are flipped in the antipoodes; months December thru March are summer. Winter cold (May thru September) blows forcefully from the southwest.

Rimu is beautiful, a little splintery. In New Zealand it's fine grain is exploited for mouldings and furniture. In the exploitative spirit of English Imperialism, resources like native woods were harvested with little regard to sustainabliity. The astonishingly majestic Kaori forests  were reduced to frail remnants to make superb ships for Britain's wooden fleet.

Participating in native wood exploitation, I made my bed of Rimu, and added a heater to combat Wellington's chill.

I had ridden a bike to work in Ann arbor (10 miles one way), and in Austin (a more leisurly 4 miles each way); anticipationg a more difficult ride in New Zealand I had traded in my 10 speed for an 18 speed mountain bike. Good choice.

New Zealand had suffered an economic crisis in 1984 after the government had rationalized their ecomony. They had worked themselves into the ruinous position of subsidizing their main source of trade income. The recesson that followed rationalization meant that many of Databank's 1984 employees left for Australia or elsewhere in the English speaking world. Thus Databank was generous with incentives, making it easier for the family to pick -up and move. Databank were continuously attentive to staff morale; thus the many sponsored activities and parties. Can you believe free Steinlager at Friday staff meetings?

wrote Jan. 9, 1986 of fighting gorse, changed diet, Chinese dishes, Pat, Matthew, Kate and Kris. +Context

15 Lynmouth Ave.
Karori, Wellington, New Zealand

Clifton Sunset Home
Clifton, TX

Dear father,

I’ve been out in the garden. (what we would call the yard in the states) learning about Gorse. Gorse covers much of the hillsides, reclaiming the land from the grass. Throughout all the winter and spring, it was in flower. It was brought from Great Britain to be used to grow animal retaining hedges. But it quickly spread. It’s covered with leaves shaped like needles. It grows thick and impenetrable. Good sheep fence, but a nuisance in the yard. Trimming it is like giving a porcupine a haircut.

Our diet is a little different here. We eat lamb now and then – almost never ate lamb before coming to New Zealand. Chicken is rather expensive, so we less of it. More fish. More apples when they are in season (in May – October) Pat is learning to cook Chinese dishes – lots of ginger and lots of rice. Matthew Kate and Kris like it. Matthew who dislikes fish voluntarily gets seconds when it’s prepared with ginger.

I enclose a picture of Kate. She’s standing on a rocky shore.

Love.

Walton.

Letter's Context

2026-09-28


We, Pat, Matt, Kate, Kris and I visited Clyde, Louise, Bette and Embrey in West Germany in the autumn of 1984 to say goodbye, we intended to move to New Zealand. Pat and I feared a nuclear war could erupt between the US and the USSR.

Bette had laid out an elaborate itinerary; using The Royal Corps of Transport "Berliner" to traverse Soviet occupied East Germany to reach Berlin. sometime between 8 and 14 September. All nine of us went along. Clyde, I think, depended on his wheel chair.

We crossed thrugh checkpoint Charlie to go to a museum of antiquities in the Soviet sector of Berlin where, quite by chance we happened upon Nancy and her husband, an intelligence Lt Col in the Air Force. Nancy and Pat had worked together in the Zilker PTA. Nancy invited us to dinner.

Their quarters were in a very uppper crust neighborhood in West Berlin. Before Kristalnacht, the house had been owned by wealthy Jews.

By December 1986, 15 months later, Father's declining capacity and increasing need for care overwhelmed Mother's capacity for careing. Her declining health continued to reduce that capacity .Their satisficing way of dealing with the problem was to entrust Clyde's care with the Clifton Sunset Home  . In some ways, this choice was optimal because Clyde knew some of the residents from childhood and that satisfied some of his need for social connection.

wrote Feb. 3, 1986 of pronouncing "R's",old typewriter, end of Christmas/summer holiday. +Context

15 Lynmouth Ave.
Karori, Wellington, New Zealand

Georg Buchner 4,
6236 Eschborn 2
W Germany

Summer vacation is coming to an end and we are ever so glad; the children will have to go to school again. They will have something to focus on besides making themselves and us unhappy. Several nights ago at dinner was typical.

Matthew was teasing Kris about her "R" sounds. She pronounces R's like W's. Roger comes out "Wogew", rabbit like "wabbit". I've tried to assist in the past, pointing out that I didn't know what a wabbit is, did she mean rabbit? I scolded Matthew for teasing her, insisting that my efforts -were aimed at trying to help her to encourager her to work on her R sounds. He insisted with genuine indignation, "Don't you think that's what I'm trying to do?" That must have been an almost identical repeat of the pattern of my teasing Bette about eating. I tried to compose a mini-lecture in my mind, finding by observation what was the distinction between an R and a W. All I could observe was that the location of the vibration for the W is at pursed lips, while the R 's vibration seems to be in the cavity between tongue and front teeth: I was In the process of delivering the mini lecture when Pat interrupted (she's always spoiling things by interrupting) "She just said it". "Said what?" said I.

Say it again, Kris. Once again through tears "The rabbit went to the restroom in the restaurant". Halting, but quite acceptable R's. Then Kate, not to be outdone repeated the same. Perfectly good

You may have guessed, we got a typewriter. Its an old IBM, pre golf ball variety only thing different, besides driving on the left side of the paper is a £ sign. Another indication of its vintage.

Things seem to last longer here. More likely they are made to last longer. Yesterday we saw a Cord  on the road. Its not uncommon to see several pre WW II vehicles on the road. There are also a lot of strange looking one. Singer, Citroen, Holden, Talbot, Wolsley, Zephyr. American cars of any vintage are relatively rare. Among recent cars, Japanese seem to have the edge in numbers.

Wellington has pretty much returned. Just after Christmas the town seemed to have been decimated. Many shops were closed, the streets relatively deserted. Only with the store sales of the latter part of January did the down town streets refill. Now with school upon us the road to Wellington proper and the suburban shops are full again. When Christmas and summer vacation coincide, there is too little reason to stay.

2026-10-9

Kate and Kris went to Karori Normal School  ; we could see the school and yard below from our kitchen nook or the dining room deck. Only in the worst weather did Pat need to take them in the red Toyota Corolla. Usually the girls could go to the far end of Lynmouth and take the stairs and path down to Burrows Avenue, thence to Karori Rd and the school.

Matt went to Wellington College  ; it required a bus trip to the Mount Victoria suburb. Matt wore the school uniform.

wrote Mar. 17, 1986 of Matthew, Kate, Kris, activities, Pat Tyler, Peg Gilman, Sandy, David Humphrey.. +Context

15 Lynmouth Ave..
Karori, Wellington 5, New Zealand

2114 Tierney Ave.
Ft Worth, TX

Dear Eve,

Thanks for remembering all of our dates (anniversary, etc.) if you will send me a list of yours (Mike’s birthday, your anniversary) I will add them to the calendar and maybe by some miracle I’ll remember to send cards. It is always nice to be remembered, isn’t it?

Life has become pretty routine around here. The kids are back in school – Matthew seems happy at Wellington college and the girls like being back at Karori normal. (I’m sorry if I repeat things – I forget who I’ve told what – I may have even told you that). The street here is full of little girls and K & K love it! They go from house to house to play – sometimes they all eat lunch here – sometimes in their clubhouse (a playhouse behind the Conway‘s house). Anyway, they have a ball – bicycling, skating, trampolining (lots of people have tramps here). I’ve been running – with Pat Tyler (our first neighbor) or by myself. I entered a fun run on Sunday and ran 9.5 km in 47 minutes 30 seconds. Not great, but I was very pleased with me. There is going to be a half marathon in June and I’ve been thinking about training for it. Probably won’t but it’s fun to think that it’s possible.

I’m gradually making a few good friends. This morning I’m going shopping with Sandy – a lady from England. She’s quite nice – has lived all over England, spent nine years in Singapore and a short while in New York. Now she and family are in New Zealand – her husband is Swiss and is the manager of a hotel here in Wellington (the best one). Also, we see the Gilman semi often. They moved here from Boston and have a boy Matthews age – Nathaniel – who also goes to Wellington College. Peg (mom) is OK and since we share a lot in common a lot of common experiences, we talk often – but she probably wouldn’t be one of my favorite people in the US. The Conway’s (across the street) are very nice. Marijka and Roger have three girls and they’re just a bit younger than we are. She’s lots of fun. I’ve been thinking about getting a part-time job or going to school (for what I don’t know.) I’m not in a hurry, but I do feel I’m ready to move on from the kitchen you know. If I stay home all day, I may have to clean the house or something just as boring. We’ll see.

David Humphrey (from Austin) finished his book about the history of Austin. It’s a big, beautiful well written book, which we were pleased to get – an autographed copy from for Christmas. It also got good reviews. Did you see it in the stores – or is it just in Austin – surely not.

We enjoyed the pictures you sent – anytime you have an extra – We’ll be happy to get one. We’ve been busy here at the house – putting trees, shrubs, etc., so I haven’t taken any pictures lately.

It’s been a pleasant summer here – although it’s still hard for me to think “summer“ and that next is Autumn. I guess it takes a few years.

I hope you’re all in good health and happy. Say hello to everyone. Love

Pat.

Do write!

wrote Apr. 21, 1986 of Hazel, garden planting, Matthew, Kate and Kris, Walt in Nelson, visit to US around Christmas, '87.. +Context

15 Lynmouth Ave..
Karori, Wellington 5, New Zealand

2114 Tierney Ave.
Ft Worth, TX

Dear Eve,

We were sorry to hear about Mike’s mother. I hope things have settled down and you and Mike are feeling better. (Walt intends to write – but he hasn’t yet.”) Thanks for sending the newspaper article about her death. Was I wrong to feel that Mike’s dad should have been mentioned? After all, they spent many years together – it just seemed to me he should have been listed as a survivor or is this what modern living means?

I will change the subject now – but we do share your grief.

Life continues here – Autumn is not much different from summer – we still have beautiful sunny days, but on the days of the Southerlies (your norther) it is quite a bit colder. I’m so glad we have lots of windows facing north – the sun streams in all day And we are protected from the cold South winds by a wall and the garage door. We have planted over 30 (small) trees. The people who lived here before us didn’t plant one tree. We’ve planted apple, pear, fig, a native rimu , a Puka (once a sacred Māori tree) several cabbage trees – (they are of the Lilly family and look a lot like a palm trees, a purple wattle that looks silvery. I got that for Walt for our silver anniversary) several (fern trees) and dozens of pheballium) to line the side of our section and protect us from some of the winds). Walt has been looking for a pecan tree - also a walnut. It seems that every Saturday find us at a nursery, although we did get the cabbage trees and more free (someone was thinning out his grove).

We (rather the kids) are almost at the end of their first trimester. Matthew, who seems to enjoy Wellington college, had a whole series of test last week. He did best in Latin and French and science. Maths wasn’t as easy as he thought and the one in social sciences threw him for a loop – but not next time he’ll know more what to expect. He is on a soccer team, is trying to get on the cross country team, (running) and will start playing in a brass ensemble soon. He says he feels very much a “Yankee nerd“ (as some kids used to call him) but is feeling more part of the group. his best friend here is a boy from Western Samoa.

Walt went to a city (town) called Nelson in the South Island. He said it is very much like Taos, there are a lot of writers, artists, potters, Silversmith, etc. He really enjoyed being there – (databank has an office there – and Walt flew down for a few days, just to get an idea of the operation there). We all hope to go soon. Did I tell you we may get to come to the USA for Christmas in 87. After two years, Walt gets a month off (in addition to his regular vacation time) plus money (extra months salary) in the form of travel vouchers. So it looks like we can come and spend 4 - 6 weeks while the kids are on summer vacation.

Kate and Kris did enjoy the pictures of the cats that Amy sent (they are on the walls in their room). I’m sorry they didn’t say anything – but, they showed everyone in the neighborhood the pictures. They are still taking violin and cello lessons at Wellington High School – paid for by the city –. They are getting better but still have ups and downs about the whole thing.

We went to see (and hear) Carl Sagan at the University last week. Out of room and news. Hope to hear from you soon.

Love Pat.

Letter's Context

2026-10-30

The sun streamed in so much that some exposed fabric was bleached and weakened. Ditto book jackets. Cooler ambient temperatures and commonly windy conditions cancelled our awareness of the sun's intensity. Then too, the protective ozone layer was more damaged in the southern hemisphere than in the northern. Meaning, we probably experienced more damaging UV rays than we would have in the northern hemisphere.

Gardening on level areas (Lower Hutt, 123 Campbell in Karori, had looked so easy. So many homes displayed delicate flowering plants. The steep slope of 15 Lynmouth, higher wind speeds and possibly its architectural, levelling fill material made for less forgiving gardening. I had a pretty high failure rate. I had no appreciation for the dessicating effects of wind.

The Databank travel bonuses were more of Databank's employee retention measures. New Zealand had long been an exporter of talent; many young wanted to free themselves from the insularity and separation of the far southwest Pacific, if only for a few years.

Karori, Wellington 5, New Zealand

2114 Tierney Ave.
