wrote Nov. 20, 1988 of Walt in Ft Worth, Kris injuring arm, 2 Thanksgiving dinners, Matthew's finals.

15 Lynmouth Ave..
Karori, Wellington 5, New Zealand

2114 Tierney Ave.
Ft Worth, TX

Dear Eve,

Before I forget – don’t send anything to New Zealand. We hope to be back in the states within the next few months. And besides Walt will be in Fort Worth around the 20th or 21st of December. He will be looking for a job on the west coast but will come there to see his parents. I’m sure he will call to see you at least for a short time. Don’t know when we are coming, but we do have someone very interested in this house – whether they are interested as we want them to be is another story.

Kris and Kate went off to a week long camp on 7 November. After an hour there, Kris accidentally put her hand through a glass door. That necessitated an ambulance trip back to Wellington (about 60 miles). Two hours in surgery, and the plaster cast for her arm. Seems she cut an artery, tendon, and a nerve. The artery was completely severed, but was an unimportant one and was tied off. Fortunately the nerve and tendon were not completely severed so alignment was not a problem. Seems she’s very lucky – the Surgeon can’t see that she has lost any feeling or movement in her hand or fingers. The plaster comes off in three more weeks meanwhile, Kate spent the rest of the week at camp and Kris was in the hospital for three days. Kate was jealous that Kris got flowers, magazines, etc. but Kris didn’t complain too much about missing camp

Glad the birthday party went well – it sounded like fun.

We are going to have two Thanksgiving dinners this year. One I’ll cook for some Argentinian friends who have heard about these dinners but never had one. The other one we will have with the Gilman’s - fellow Americans, whose time it is to buy the turkey – quite an undertaking over here (I’m getting a big chicken only about $15 rather than $60 for the turkey). My sister sent pecans for the events.

Kate’s picture is enclosed, but Kris missed school the day the pictures were taken – so look at Kate‘s twice (Kate does have on Kris‘s sweater).

Matthew is busy studying for his finals which begin this week. He’s had this week and last week off from school to study. I just read Tom Robbins jitterbug perfume – it was funny. Right now I’m reading life and death in Shanghai – a true story written by Mien Cheng about the cultural revolution in China.

Hello to everyone

Love

Pat PS if there’s anything you want from New Zealand you’d better let me know soon.

wrote Nov. 24, 1988 of Pat & Matthew final exams, Rimutaka rafting trip, Louise still weak, and Thanksgiving dinner.

15 Lynmouth Ave..
Karori, Wellington 5, New Zealand

2114 Tierney Ave.
Ft Worth, TX

Dear Eve,

We have less than three weeks to go before we head back for the states. We are all getting excited – Kate and Kris talk of little else. Matthew is also excited, but he is sitting exams at his school this week and has been busy studying. These are fourth form exams and every fourth former in New Zealand sits the same test at the same time. Matthew has English, science, French, maths, history and music.

I haven’t heard the results of my two exams yet - probably won’t for another week or so.

Walt and Matthew went on a white water raft trip a week or so ago – they had a good time. K and K and I didn’t go because the age limit is 14 years old – and I’m not 14.

I read those Margaret Truman mysteries a while back – they are good – yesterday I saw a book by Elliot, Roosevelt – detective genre with Mrs. Roosevelt as the lead. Have you heard about those? There are at least two of them – and according to book jacket had good reviews. I’ve been reading lots of fiction the last couple of weeks, but nothing noteworthy. Did read Michener’s Texas a couple of months back.

Today we are up in the clouds. We’ve had really beautiful weather lately, but today I look out the window and see a wall of thick white cloud. This doesn’t happen in Wellington too often – and it’s Eerie.

Walt’s Mother is doing better but still is very weak. She says she has trouble walking. My mother is much the same. How are your parents and Mike’s dad getting along?

In the front of our house we have a small enclosed courtyard, which looks lovely right now - all the roses are in bloom – we’ve had fresh roses in the house for several weeks now. The trees Walt planted are growing – some better than others. We finally did find a pecan tree (pronounced pikin by the garden centre manager) but unfortunately, can’t find any pickins in the store – so we won’t have pecan pie for Thanksgiving. we will have 14 people here on Sunday after Thanksgiving for the traditional dinner - all Americans - did you cook? Did Julie come? We will have pumpkin pie – and of course the turkey – which will cost over $50. And it’s our time to buy the bird this year!

Must go now - enjoy the article.

Love.

Pat

wrote Dec. 8, 1988 of Portland, jobs, NZ departure tax, Northern California, return flight, and film developing.

15 Lynmouth Ave.
Karori, Wellington, New Zealand

Dearest Pat, Matthew, Kate and Kris,

I miss you. Missing you got so bad. I took mother‘s present out and put up your pictures. Pat’s doesn’t have a frame.

Portland is beautiful. Trees still show their color, but it doesn’t seem to get too cold here; I saw two monkey puzzle trees – quite large ones. The city strides the convergence of the Willamette and Columbia rivers. The southwest side looks down upon the other 3/4 of the city.

But prospects don’t look very good here. I’ve left a few resumes. I’ll post some applications for Oregon state and some federal jobs, but the opportunities look a little thin.

Leaving Auckland, I was shocked to find that New Zealand charges a $10 departure tax. When you fly out, be sure to have at least $40 in Auckland.

The drive up through northern California was beautiful. Four or five times the highway became very twisted as it slithered its way among huge redwoods. Some almost as wide as Tane Mahuta and most far taller. Northern California is a much different place than the lower 2/3 seemingly remote much of the land undulating like New Zealand around Wellington. More trees here though.

I got my return flight changed to return to San Francisco on the 28th. That way I can spend Christmas with mother and Betty. I’ll call them tonight to see if it works out. I reschedule the return to Auckland on February 25. Don’t expect to use it, but if no job and no sale by then I may as well return.

You can write me at 4710 if you like. And relay messages through Mother & Bette I hope. I’ll call tonight to see if it’s OK. Relatively sure it will be.

Matthew, have you developed the film go ahead, read the instructions

  1. practice loading the developer reel in the dark
  2. use the bar – close the door, do it at night - dining room, snug lights out to load the film in the dark maybe try one at a time
  3. mix the top bath
  4. cover loaded reel then you can work in light in laundry room
  5. use 9 1/2 ounces of chemicals for one roll. Be sure you load the bottom reel in just one roll. Otherwise it takes 16 ounces to cover the top reel.
  6. Measure temperature first record the temperature and consult the time chart.
  7. Poor developer in and time it. Be sure to swirl our twist the reel every 30 seconds.
  8. When time is up poor developer back into its container.
  9. Rinse with water, film and measure
  10. measure and poor in stop bath
  11. Time it
  12. return stop back to container
  13. let film dry in protected area say closet
  14. you’ll need a pair of clothes pins, one at top one at bottom for weight let it dry

Love

Walt

wrote Dec. 8, 1988 of downtown Portland, Powell's bookstore and fax.

15 Lynmouth Ave.
Karori, Wellington, New Zealand

Dearest Pat, Matt, Kate and Kris,

It isn’t often I write two letters in a day to the same person (s). But I wanted to share with you the delight I found today.

There’s a little knot of downtown, West Portland, tightly circumscribed by a loop of interstate freeway. It seems to be the single ‘head’ of Portland. Banks, city, state, Federal, multinational offices are found there, along with posh stores, a university, churches, the city/County library and some industry. Parking is almost impossible in the head; red meters are for 10 minutes, Green 30 and Blue 90. I found a blue meter at the 10 o’clock position and began a walk of about 20 city blocks toward the center, casually glancing at the shops and people along the way. One café was so incongruous that I broke my stride to look more fully, then came to a full stop. A café, full of people sitting at tables, sipping coffee. But they were all, men, women, girls, and boys reading as they noshed. I looked further, saw a modest food, service counter, and lots of book displays. Not a café, but Powell‘s bookstore.

On my return trip I stopped to go in and realized it occupies a whole city block! A little odd, I trapsed through caverns of ancient rickety bookshelves testing the weight of new titles.

I knew I had no business there, I have applications to complete, resumes to send, another city to visit tomorrow, but of course I had to tramp over to the nosh corner to examine their wares. I bought nothing. I just realized this is a good place to live.

Thanks for the fax Pat. It cost $15 on my end; must’ve been $45 on your end. I hope not more. You did great for such short notice.

Federal jobs look tight. How about Vancouver, Washington, Denver or Yuma?

Love.

Walt.

wrote Dec 12, 1988 of Seattle, views, Pike Street Market, Olympic Peninsula, and jobs.

15 Lynmouth Ave.
Karori, Wellington, New Zealand

Dearest Pat, Matt, Kate and Kris,

Seattle is big. And prosperous. And wet. Imagine what the Marlborough Sound would look like if all of New Zealand’s population were crushed together within 50 miles of Picton. Islands become suburbs linked by floating bridges and ferries. Saturday I walked around the inner city. O. K. – Nice views of Puget sound ferries toot as they berth. Pike Street Market was special. Three levels of stalls and shops under roof. More stalls spilling out into the street under uncertain skies. Fruit, seafood, crafts, flowers, vegetables, jewelry, leather, wood, honey, clothes, and a crush of colorful, good humored people. One fish vendor, over supplied with crabs, started giving them away. Do you have any idea how long it takes to cook crab in a motel shower?

Sunday I drive around to the Olympic Peninsula, for sightseeing. Not a smart thing when rain threatened Seattle, for there it was a certainty on the Olympic Peninsula. I drove by Boeing. Lots of airliners and other civilian craft. Around Bremerton, there were three small aircraft carriers and a few destroyers and cruisers in dock.

I took a short walk in the rain in an Olympic peninsula Forest. The quantity of mushrooms was amazing. So it’s no surprise. I’ve got a bad case of athletes foot.

There are a few jobs in the paper. They all say “send your résumé“. I’ll send 10 today and hopefully they call mother. – I put her address on the resumes.

Gotta go to the post office. Since it’s near Christmas that may take the rest of the day.

Love.

Walt.

wrote Dec. 28, 1988 of isolation, Mike, Vi, Mikey and Susan, Mary, Jimmy, Clara and Louise.

4710 E Loraine
Fort Worth, TX

15 Lynmouth Ave.
Karori, Wellington, New Zealand

Dear Matthew, Kate, Kris, and Pat,

I would like to tell you what I see that is New in America. Of course the thing I view most constantly the thing I view as most changed, is me.

After 17 days of meticulous, scientific measurement, and observation I am propelled to the conclusion that the main change is the lack of you, I am without the four of you. This may seem a trivial observation, but you must be aware that this simple unity of this observation was masked by, experiential diversity.

At first, for example, I was contentedly lulled by the deceptive appeal of the peace and quiet. I haven’t heard arguments for weeks. – Well more precisely when I came to the US there were no arguments for weeks. Suddenly, realizing I might expire without engaging in a good argument, I made a mad dash across the continental divide to the nearest friend with whom I can argue, Mike Jurica.

Well, not really, not just to argue more to be with familiar friendly faces. Mike and Vi were familiar, but Mikey was much changed and Susan I really never knew before.

Mikey looked really good. He’s taller than his father and has a ready, charming smile. He is studying finance and philosophy at the university of New Mexico at Las Cruces. Mikey beat me the first game of chess. I pulled the second one out only because I had done a little study of end games. I was happy to let the two Mike’s battle out the next games. Apparently he skis well. All the Jurica‘s do. When one member of the family works at the ski lodge (Mike at Sipapu,) all members get free lift passes. So Mike Junior skis well enough to work as a ski instructor during the Christmas break,

Mike Senior has made many changes to the house. There is a massive spiral stone staircase up to the second level. (They used a ladder to get up before) above the kitchen is Mikey’s room. Mike Senior put in a herringbone patterned wood ceiling. He built a large multi sash window and put in an adobe floor. Susan‘s room is above Mike and Vi’s. I slept there the night of the 18th to the schools aquarium air pump burble all night long.

After Llano de San Juan, I went to Fort Worth. It helps to be with familiar people. The loneliness gets oppressive.

Mary and Jimmy told me Clara is very unwell. Her kidneys are malfunctioning again. Jimmy explained that kidney damage tends to be progressive. That is under some circumstances the rate of damage can accelerate. He did not expect her to survive this episode.

My mother, Louise, is very slow and deliberate in her movements. She rests a lot and uses me as her arms and legs. I’ve been washing dishes and cooking a little in addition to a few repairs around the house.

Please be kind to each other and after that be kind to each other and next importantly, be kind to each other.

Love,

Walton

wrote Dec. 28, 1988 of Louise, Clyde, Bette, Embrey, bus San Francisco to Taos, Mike and Vi Jurica.

4710 E Loraine
Fort Worth, TX

15 Lynmouth Ave.
Karori, Wellington, New Zealand

My Dear Pat, Matthew, Kate, Kris,

Everyone seems well here; mother is extremely slow, slow, tires quickly; but she wants to go when she feels able. So we went to Clifton yesterday after the stop at the medical lab and the pharmacy, and the house and the pharmacy again.

Clyde seemed to recognize me. At first, I believe, he thought I was his father. “Dad“ He questioned, and a look of gratitude and delight flickered momentarily in his face. Eventually, he seemed to recognize Mother, Embrey and me, but he had great difficulty forming sentences that connected with his feelings. His foot was reputedly better.

Bette looks good! She seems to adhere to her diet. We made ZIMPHSTIMMERN night before last. Quite simple. 6 cups of ground nuts, two egg whites, cup of sugar, tablespoon of cinnamon “the ZIMPH“. Roll 1/4 inch cut into stars “STIMMERN“ cover with icing and bake ten minutes. (this is not a reliable recipe; don’t try it; it’s just to show Bette has not lost interest in rich food.)

Embrey sang last night in a children’s choir; about us 30 children from her private school sang for their dinner at an East Fort Worth country club. (Actually, we did not stay to eat; it would have cost the adults eight dollars each, and there was nothing there that Bette our mother could eat. So we left after the program.) One nervous little girl on the front row, added two visual crescendoes of projectile vomiting to the middle of the program, but everyone kept going on as if nothing had happened.

The bus ride from San Francisco was an ordeal. It was good to see the Jurica’s. Mike and Vi are much unchanged. Mike still has relatively disappointing efforts in sales and and intermittent connection with the ski resort, Sipapu. Vi seems solidly connected to the school in Peñasco. I’ll write more about Mikey and Susie later.

Love, Walton.

1988 <———> 1989
wrote Jan. 1, 1989 of Rocky raccoon, Juricas, Louise, Bette, Embrey, Annie Joy, Sharon Sue, Sussie, Donna and Horst Krause.

4710 E Loraine
Fort Worth, TX

15 Lynmouth Ave.
Karori, Wellington, New Zealand

My dearest Kris, Kate, Pat, and Matthew,

Rocky very nearly became a Tucumcari, New Mexico mascot. Twice Greyhound lost him for a day or more. Once on the way from San Francisco to Taos, he, along with the rest of my luggage, was lost for the duration of my stay with the Jurica’s. Then, on the trip from Taos to Fort Worth, Greyhound lost him again. I got my luggage the next day, but it’s been a rocky trip for Rocky.

Ill health slows your grandmother. She walks, mostly with the aid of a four footed Walker. A sleepy, infant crawls faster than she walks. She no longer accomplishes much. It taxes her to stand or even sit upright for long. Embrey never straightens up or picks up things about the house. Bette ditto. Consequently, the house was more disorganized than usual, if that be possible. Embrey liked the candy. Bette helped herself to the Apricot Royale and pronounced it the best candy bar she had ever eaten.

Embrey also got, among other things, a chess set. She and I must have played 20 games. Her game is improving quickly.

Bette still likes to eat; we’ve been to Nirvana‘s thrice and the Bangkok once. I made pizza once, using turkey and pineapple for the first time. Turkey no, pineapple, yes.

One of the times we went out to eat, we went with Annie Joy, my aunt, Sharon Sue, Annie’s daughter, and Johnathon, Sharon‘s three year-old son. They all looked well.

On another dinner, one on Bette‘s birthday, my old neighbors Susie and Donna came too. (among many others). I really didn’t recognize Donna, and wasn’t embarrassed to admit it. Earlier in the week, I saw another old neighbor, (and I’m talking 30 years ago) Horst Krause, I didn’t really recognize him either, except for where he was.

I want to hug you all.

Walt.

wrote Jan 15, 1989 of Oregon schools, SAT, public library, and answering service.

4195 S.W. Cedar Hills Blvd.
Beaverton, OR 97005

15 Lynmouth Ave.
Karori, Wellington, New Zealand

Dear Kris, Pat, Matthew, and Kate,

I am encouraged by what I read of Oregon schools. I wanted to know, “how do Oregon schools compare with schools in the rest of the country, and within the state which schools are best?”

Well, they’re probably are no unarguable answer answers to that question, but what information I can find encourages me for you, Chris, Matt, and Kate.

The closest I could think of an objective measure was comparative SAT scores. Scholastic aptitude test (SAT‘) are taken by most high schoolers headed for college. And Oregon students score higher than any other states students, except for New Hampshire. At least that’s what the Oregonian wrote. But the college board, the institution that administer the test, doesn’t publicize school district score averages. The board just informs each school board. What appears in the 87 September 23 issue of the Oregonian (newspaper) is that Eugene is better than Salem is better than Beaverton is better than the state average better than Portland. There was no information for Medford.

There is, I suppose, some teaching for SAT. So it would be well, Matt, for you to start here soon – or work with a SAT provider. High SAT scores are useful for getting into University.

I really liked the public library. What was best about it was how much it is used. Late Sunday afternoon the library seemed to be at least 1/3 to 1/2 capacity right up to closing time.

Now that I have an answering service address I hope you write me

Love.

Walt,

4195 S.W. Cedar Hills Blvd.,

Beaverton, OR 97005

(503) 627–0383.

I’ll probably stay at this motel till 1/29/89

My dearest Pat, Mathew, Kate and Kris,

Well Monday wasn't as bad as I though. It's Martin Luther King's birthday but only federal, state, county employees are observ- ing it so can get something done today after all. I completed the application with Standard Insurance, and talked with the name Jeannifer gave So far it sounds hopeful. Tonight at 7 I expect: to get a call from the man in Medford. May get a job in Medford, may only get a trip to Medford, may only get a conver— sation. l' Il find out.

Got rid of the Oregon Rental car. It cost $132.00 per week and I didn't use it anywhere near what f 'thought. I'll rely on cabs and public trans— port for the remainder of the time.- Thought to cut living expenses by moving to cheaper accommodations, but the next step is a Long step it seems. The rooms looked so grubby that feared depression would overwhelm me if tried living is some of the places I've seen. It really makes me think about all the homeless people one sees on the street

It's a painful anomaly that there are so many homeless people in what was the most wealthy country in the world. Ronnie ameliorated the problem slightly by making America other than the wealthiest country in the world. Now the anomaly is a little less painful, but there are more homeless on the streets than ever before. This is Stanley Dannis's typewriter the typewriter above cost 25ø for 10 minutes in the libraries. They are really very nice; good print, super correction facility, but not free, and not available When you want them.

wrote Jan 20, 1989 of travel from San Francisco to Portlandby air, or train

Portland, OR

Dear Matthew, Kate, and Kris

I Just now had time to look into your travel options. To my mind the best option is Amtrack It would work as follows:

1) Arriva in S. F. at 3:10 PM This is Thursday; rush hour traffic is coming up. You could try to rush into town on the Airporter bus ($3.5O per person, )but with all the luggage you'll have, you may as well wait at the airport until 6:30 or 7:00. Have a leisurely dinner at the Airport, rent a cart to hold your luggage or put it in a locker temporarily. Airports are nicer places to wait than train stations.

2) Between 6:30 and 7:00, or even as late as 7:30, take the airporter bus to Trans Bay Bug Terminal. (Tell the driver you want to go to the San Francisco Amtrack station. You will have manage your luggage. With Matthew there the 4 of you can do it easily. This Is really a bus terminal with an Amtrack ticketing office. You want to get here after 7:00 PM to avoid the rush hour crush sinceyou'll have to wrestle with luggage, I can prepay for the tickets, you should be able to pick them up by confirming name and reservation number (136289). You can check in your luggage at the Amtrack desk. Then a bus will take you on a short ride (should be no more than 30 min) to Oakland. This ie an Amtrack bus, not a city bus so you don't have to worry about getting the right stop. The bus leaves San Francisco for Oaklend at 8:20.

3) In Oakland you get on the train. I've reserved a delux bedroom end 4 paesaqes, The delux bedroom sleeps 3 (maybe four If Kate and Kris can share a bed. Breakfast and lunch come with the bedrooms. I can't get an economy bedroom (sleeps

2),they are all taken. You sleep overnight, breakfast end lunch on the train, watch Oregon scenery (beautiful, I'm told) pass by, end get in Portland at 2:20 pm

wrote Feb. 13, 1989 of Portland, Powell;s bookstore, Matthew, Kate and Kris's schools, house purchase and motel living. +Context

8905 S W 30th Ave
Portland, OR 97219

2114 Tierney Ave.
Ft Worth, TX

Dear Eve,

You sure got that letter here fast. It’s nice to know that you’ll get this the same week. I write it. We are enjoying getting to know Portland. I didn’t realize it was so big. Or so hilly. We spent Saturday driving around looking at the views. Then went to the science museum for a few hours. Then ended up at Powell‘s bookstore, which takes up a whole city block. I love that store. I was there two hours and only to the C’s in the literature section. I think they must have every new book ever printed – not quite, but the selection was tremendous.

Matthew is enrolled in 10th grade at Woodrow Wilson, High School and Kate and Kris are at Robert Gray Middle School. They, K and K, were pleased to find out that they change classes and have lockers just like high school. Walt researched the area pretty well before we came so we know where and what schools we wanted for the kids. We tried very hard to find a house for rent in the area, but there was nothing. So we took the plunge rather quickly and bought a house. It’s in such a nice well- maintained area just three blocks from Matthew’s school and about seven from K & K’s. It’s on the bus line and the hike and bike trail is very close. Walt can bike to work on the trail and not get on a street until he’s practically at the office. The only problem is that we can’t move in until March 15 or so. We put in an offer and it was accepted, but we have to wait for a house inspection (tomorrow) and then the people will start getting ready to leave. They have been there since the house was built (1960) so it will take them a while. Settlement date is March 15. We’ll be glad. We are living in one room (five of us) (six counting cat) with two double beds, rollaway bed, a table, two chairs, a fridge, stove, sink, TV, chest, 10 pairs of shoes, 11 suitcases, etc., etc. So we find a lot of things to do on the weekends so we can get out. We found Kris in the other room – the bathtub that’s OK until someone needs to use the facilities.

We are all glad to be here – it feels good to be back – even if it’s not all the way back.

Love.

Pat

Dear Eve,

We get to move into our new house on Saturday – and we can’t wait! This room gets smaller each day. Five people and a cat need more room. The people we bought the house from are really nice. Tell everyone hello – sorry K & K & M are such poor letter writers – maybe when we get into the house –

wrote Mar. 31, 1989 of adapting to no furniture, no food ware, Walt’s mother visiting April 20.

6907 S. W. 13th Ave.
Portland, OR 97219

2114 Tierney Ave.
Ft Worth, TX

Dear Eve,

We’ve been in our new house a little over two weeks now and find it very comfortable. That’s in spite of the fact that our furniture won’t arrive from New Zealand until June. We bought the kids mattresses which are on the floor in their rooms. We bought a hide-a-bed from the previous owners , and Walt and I dragged the mattress up to our bedroom. Our clothes are either hanging in the closet or folded on the shelf of the closet. We bought den furniture from the Evans also and the kitchen table and chairs. Aside from that, the fridge, washer, dryer, and TV; The house is empty. I think I could get used to this minimalist existence. If only we had a few drawers. We’ve gone to several garage sales to buy silverware and pots and pans. (found a really nice seven piece set of porcelain covered ones – you know the ones painted white with little flowers) for $12. Also, we’ve gotten some nice big hanging pots of plants for a couple of dollars each. I found you a little blue jar – some sort of apothecary one about 4 inches tall with a lid (the same color) that acts as a cup for the medicine(I presume). I’ll bring it when we come – if we come this summer. The kids and I are planning to come, but we don’t know when or how at this point.

Matthew got his learners permit two weeks ago, starts drivers education next week. I don’t know if I’m ready for this stage.

We like the area where we live – It’s really convenient. Our Dictionary has not come yet so I can’t check on my spelling). I started running last week – just around the neighborhood and it’s really a nice neat area. Matthew walks to school (it’s closer to his high school than Zilker was to our old house in Austin) and sometimes comes home for lunch. The shopping area is just a few minutes away and the doctor and dentist are within a five minute walk. K & K’s school is a little further – maybe 10 to 15 minutes by foot – but there’s a busy street between our house and the school. The school bus, however, stops on the next corner.

It is really pretty up here. Have you been to Portland? We took a drive through the Columbia River Gorge on Easter. The waterfalls, snow covered mountains and river valleys are as spectacular as New Zealand.

Our house is 30 years old, but very well-maintained. And the best part – or my favorite part of the house is the built-in desk in the kitchen where I’m sitting now. Of course Matthew loves having a bedroom and bath down in the basement. He comes up to eat when he’s hungry – which is quite often. Will enclose real estate picture of house.

Say hello to all. How are the New Zealand plants coming along? Walt’s mother is coming to visit on April 20 for a few weeks. Kate and Kris go to the sixth grade camping trip on the ninth – will be gone for a week. Kris is a little apprehensive since she only lasted a few hours at camp in New Zealand before she put her hands through that door. Write.

Love Pat.

wrote May 17, 1989 of beautiful spring, Louise's visit, furniture arriving, Pat & Bryan Tyler coming, running with Jackie. +Context

6907 S. W. 13th Ave.
Portland, OR 97219

2114 Tierney Ave.
Ft Worth, TX

Dear Eve,

It has been really beautiful here the past month everywhere rhododendrons and azaleas have been in bloom. Even our yard has been very colorful, reds, oranges, yellow, whites; the cherry trees were the first to bloom. Walt’s mom was here for a couple of weeks. We took her to see Mount Hood, the Pacific Ocean and the Columbia River Gorge. We also ate fresh Chinnook salmon which was out of this world. Fish is so fresh and inexpensive here so we eat a lot of it. The kids think we eat too much of it. Our furniture came while Louise was here. It was nice to see our old junk again. And the wall unit Walt made out of New Zealand wood looked great here too.

I have been trying to finish the needle pointing of two chair covers (just the seats) before our friends visit from New Zealand in June. Pat and Brian [Tyler] are on their way to West Germany for a conference and are going to spend a few days with us.

Let’s see – what else. We planted two trees in the front yard; a blue spruce and a sweet gum. Will put out a few more – are looking for a horse chestnut or an oak or a pecan.

The kids are doing fine – Matthew started behind the wheel training on Monday, Kate and Kris have a science project due tomorrow. Each I s working with another friend on the projects. I’ve been running with a girl from New Zealand. We planned to run in the Cascades run on June 18 – it’s 15 K. The furthest I’ve ever run is 10k– so I hope we can do it. On Jackie‘s birthday, she and I ran downtown (53 minutes, 53 seconds) to a restaurant where Walt, the kids and his mom met us for breakfast. That was fun. Then last Saturday, we ran for an hour. We figure we have to run 80–90 minutes for the 15 K! Then I’ll sleep for a week. The kids don’t get out of school until June 14. We (kids and I) are flying into Austin (cheaper than flying to Dallas) on July 11. I haven’t figured out when we go to Fort Worth yet. How are your kids.? You? Mike?

Love,

Pat.

wrote Sep. 27, 1989 of new part-time job, Matthew's 17th birthday, job as a waiter, Scotty and Toffee, running 5k.

6907 S. W. 13th Ave.
Portland, OR 97219

2114 Tierney Ave.
Ft Worth, TX

Dear Eve,

Well, here, I am at my job. See my type. See me file – – – well I don’t actually have to file and I really don’t have to type very much. Mostly I just sit here while the boss is out – – – – answer the phones, direct people to the right agency, open the mail. None of this is very challenging and it does not pay very well either, but the hours are right and it is just down the road from the house (5 minute drive). I work Monday through Friday from 9 to 1 – – – I’m here by myself every day but Monday. But at least I don’t have to wash any windows or clean any floors and there’s nobody there’s nothing here to eat so I’m not tempted to feed my face. What job is good for is paying for Kris and Kate‘s braces when they get them in December or January. I really didn’t intend to take a job like this – but the opportunity was too good to pass up. And if I want a bigger challenge or a bigger salary, I still have plenty of time left during the day to look.

Yesterday was Matthew 17th birthday so we had a big birthday dinner and a cherry pie for dessert. He has a job now at a rather fancy retirement home. He started bussing, but now he’s a waiter – – – he looks very nice in his formal waiters attire. He works Friday, Saturday and Sunday nights.

By the way, tell Amy happy birthday for us – – – there has been so much going on around here in the days go by without noticing much but the essentials.

Scotty [cat] seems to have adjusted pretty well. Did I tell you that when we got back from our first trip to Texas, Toffee was sick; has feline urological syndrome, and now has to be on a special scientific diet. Surely with all of your cats you have had this happen. How do you keep the other cats from eating the special food – – How do you keep the fus cat from eating the regular food? It’s a good thing Rob, my boss is not paying me to type. It’s been a long time and typewriters are so different now – – better but different.

I know there are other things I had to say, but I can’t think of right now. Walt, Matthew and Kate are all home sick today. Walt and Matthew have bad cold and Kate has a stomachache. It’s funny that they are all sick – – usually nobody is. I ran in the Portland marathon – not the marathon part; but the 5 mile run on Sunday with my kiwi friend Jackie. She’s going to go back to New Zealand soon. I’ll miss her and will also have to find someone else to run with.

Love Pat

wrote Dec. 9, 1989 to family in Portland, OR, about Embrey's typewriter, visiting Hamilton home place, visiting Clyde in Clifton and feeding him ice cream, eating breakfast with Bette, Embey and Rex Anne. +Context

4710 E Loraine
Fort Worth, TX 76117


Dear Kate, Kris, Matt, Pat,

When I first tried Embrey's typewriter, I thought it was encoded. I typed "Dear Kate" and 'zSoa¯H01S" came out. Drat!, [I] thought this silly typewriter has the capacity to write In code, and I don't know how to turn the feature off. But thinking on it again, it occurred to me that the print wheel was mis-installed. Sure enough, with the print wheel in right, it comes out readable. The spelling correction ribbon ran—out so that doesn't work, but I hope you'll overlook my many typos.

Today's path was very familiar; old but familiar. You would recognize much of it. Out highway 183 past Cedar Park, Liberty Hill, Lampassas, to Hamilton. I climbed over the fence and walked past the stock tank that Mother and Father had built. You may remember "camping" overnight there once with Granny. It was gratifying to me to see that the pecan tree Mother had me plant there appears to be prospering. The home place was more of a shambles than ever before. Mother and her sisters used to resent, I think, that Uncle Embrey got the home place. But it meant little in real terms. He certainly had a sentimental attachment to it but it was really a liability, not an asset. He could put money into it (and I suspect he had a new roof put over it, but he ever realized much from it. It was mostly unoccupied after Grandmother Walton left. [There was an occupancy by grandmother Ethel's sister Jessie and her husband Sterling.]

I went inside a short distance, a little afraid of rattle snakes even though it was cold. What really halted me was a combination of apprehension that the floor could give way beneath me, that I would be started by any animal that fled my approach, and a feeling of pain to recall locations where my parents grandparents, aunts, uncles, cousins, etc. were still alive in my memory. Walking on to the Southwest corner of the property I saw the tank {bull dozed earthen pond] Uncle Embrey had made. It had water too, Further up the hill jack rabbits startled me twice. The grass looked relatively healthy: it was not overgrazed. The troublesome part of the fence on South line, the part adjacent to the southwest corner looked ready to fall over. The tiny house on Red Bird hill looked no worse than before. The windmill however was running uncontrollably. Very bad. The wire that stops it from running was broken, so its been running uncontrollably for who knows how 1ong. Eventuality it will run itself to destruction. I need to be there some calm day, when the wind isn't turning the mill, so I can deactivate it. But I need someone with me in case I fall off.

I got ice cream for Father. That is about the only effective interaction I have with him; feeding him much as one would an infant. It's a most curious mixture of infantile behavior (unable to feed himself, unable to converse. unable to control his bodily functions and his identity as Father, the epitome of an adult role. My adult model, in every way one recognizes person. He is that person, yet he most obviously is not. Our categories just don't hold. Adult—child these are distinct aren't they? No, in him adult and infant are one. Such is the way of life and death. Yet I still try conversation I ask him questions, tell him about visiting the home place and sometimes he responds appropriately with a "yes" or "sure good" about the ice cream or "Do you know Mom and Dad" (his Mother and Father. Or "My family's all left me") So I. don't know what to make of it. Is his experience of the world like a foggy dream. Is his mind relatively aware of the present but his tongue difficult to command, much like one's arm that has "gone to sleep", that feels funny or doesn't feel at all and doesn't work right when you try to use it. If we judge solely by his speech, we may cheat the awareness that he does have, cheat it of the knowledge he would like to have.

Bette and Embrey are fine. The three of us and Rex Anne ate breakfast out. I pitched the ball to Embrey then she to me. I still hit the ball better than she. She to wants to play chess now.


Love Walt

"Hello Kate, write Embrey soon - Embrey [Bronstad].

Letter's Context

2026-08-30


For less than a year I had a job as a programmer with Standard Insurance Company in Portland Oregon. The company and I were not a good fit; we disappointed each other. My friend, Don, Nelson, who was my Team Leader at Austin VADPC, visited us in Portland around the time of his daughter Victoria's marriage in Seattle. Don helped facilitate my return to the Austin VADPC.

I had picked-up a bit of CICS at Databank in Wellington and at Standard Ins. in Portland. The VA was embarking on a CICS based application and valued my experience, however limited.

My old neighbors, George Kegley and Nancy Lynch, loaned me their 1719 Norris street home when I first relocated from Portland Oregon to Austin in November 1989. I believe Paulette Gravois may have engineered this arrangement. As if that weren't enough generosity, they loaned me George's car to drive to visit my Father in Clifton Sunset Home and Sister at 4710 E Loraine. Pat, staying back in Portland until school semester's end, had the 79 Honda, all I had was my bicycle for transport.

Mother had died August 18 of that year; many stresses of her life, professional and familial took final toll. For decades, I suspect, heart problems had limited her vitality. Clyde had been for many months at Sunset Home, I had last seen him there in the winter of 1987-88 when my family visited the US from New Zealand. Clyde had been much more coherent then, emotive, talkative and connected. Well at home in his surroundings; he knew some of the residence of Sunset Home from childhood.

The path mentioned was familiar because my family had taken it many times, visiting from Austin to Fort Worth where many of both my kin and Pat's kin lived. We took that path, longer than the I-35 path, because it was scenic and because it connected with my childhood, albeit not Pat's.

Father joined Mother in death on the 26th of the same month, December.

The telephone replaced the letter; long distance charges from Austin to Fort Worth were far less expensive than the New Zealand to Texas calls.Unfortunately telephone calls leave no trace; we have no window on their lives.

2004

- By Walt Bronstad--  Recalling them many times I picked up Pat at the university How to be before her retirement in 2008. Sometimes I parked by the Catholic center overtime. The available spots were fewer and fewer spots for waiting. She walked from work to where I waited.

Awake

  • "Some things are too close to be seen
  • But once seen, are so important they must be said.
  •  
  • Life with you has been a poem; may there be ever
  • More stanzas. But if this moment be the last
  •  
  • This I must say: It would have been such
  • Dreadful verse with anyone but you.

I wrote this 2003-10-31 {Walt on Patricia}. I don't think I ever showed it to her. The moment never arrived; the poem lay buried in my waning web site. Daughter-in-Law Jess encouraged me to say to the bedridden, aphasic, Patricia all the things I'd never said.

Patricia dealt with her illness with denial. In her last five or so years, it was unclear just what she understood. I feared she might wonder, "why is Walt saying these things, am I about to die?" Instead of solace, I might leave terror.

The chill point is: it was never said.


 

Two people are wed for almost 63 years "… one of them is taken away and what is taken away is greater than the sum of what was there. This may not be mathematically possible; but it is emotionally possible." — Julian Barnes in his “levels of life“

The thing is – nature is so exact. It hurts exactly as much as it is worth, so in a way one relishes the pain, I think if it didn’t matter, it wouldn’t matter. — Jullian Barns

Obit

Pat's obituary ran in the Austin American Statesman   and the Ft Worth Star Telegram

Patricia Bronstad, a 40-year resident of Austin, drew her last breath on Nov 6, 2023, surrounded by family in the Knoxville, Tennessee home she shared with her husband, Walt of 63 years. She had suffered from frontotemporal dementia since 2016.

We miss her friendship, kindness, and generosity. She had a beautiful smile and a self-deprecating, mischievous sense of humor. She loved books, films, museums, chocolate, Willie Nelson, Lady Longhorn basketball, and cats. She frequently traveled with her husband, her friend Paulette Gravois, and others. She was an adventurous cook of wholesome food and of decadent desserts. A daily jogger, Pat was often running Austin’s Town Lake Trail. She ran the Capital 10k for nearly 30 years.

Born January 30, 1941 in Fort Worth to Jeff and Clara Bird, the second youngest of sixteen children, she attended Birdville (Haltom City) public schools, graduating from Birdville High with honor in 1959. She wed Gilbert (Walt) Bronstad February 24, 1961.

Pat received a BA in Sociology from UT El Paso. Following her peripatetic husband, she also attended Eastern Michigan University, University of Illinois at Chicago, University of North TX, and Victoria University of Wellington, New Zealand.

Pat worked for UT Austin from the 1990s until 1/31/2008. Her last position was Information Analyst in the College of Natural Sciences. After retirement she toured North America, Europe, and Asia.

A longtime poll-worker and volunteer for Meals-on-Wheels in multiple cities, Pat was a caring community member.. She regularly supported Planned Parenthood, Habitat for Humanity, and Doctors Without Borders.

Her children Kate, Kris, and Matt; grandchildren, Millie and Harriet “Birdie” Bronstad-Hay; and daughters-in-law Jessica Brilli and Jessica Hay cherished her and glowed in Pat’s love for them.

If you wish to make a gift in her memory, make a contribution to one of the charities mentioned, or simply buy a good novel for someone you love.

A memorial service will be held near St Olaf’s Cemetery, near Cranfills Gap, TX. February 3, 2024. For details, please email pat.b.memorial@gmail.com
